{"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_2","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,240p' /root/.codex/skills/review-code-for-security-vulnerabilities/SKILL.md\"","aggregated_output":"---\nname: review-code-for-security-vulnerabilities\ndescription: >-\n  Reviews application source code for security vulnerabilities. First works out\n  what the code actually does, then searches THIS skill library for the\n  specialised skills that match the code's security-relevant behaviour, then\n  applies those skills to audit the code and reports findings with CWE ids and\n  fixes. Use this whenever asked to review, audit, or security-check source\n  code, do a secure code review, find vulnerabilities, or check code against the\n  OWASP Top 10 or CWE. Keywords: code review, secure code review, source code\n  audit, find vulnerabilities, security assessment of code, OWASP, CWE, SQL\n  injection, weak cryptography, hardcoded secrets, insecure deserialization,\n  XSS, SSRF, authentication and session flaws, JWT verification, password\n  storage.\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: application-security\ntags:\n  - code-review\n  - security-review\n  - owasp\n  - cwe\n  - sast\n  - dispatcher\nversion: \"1.1\"\nlicense: Apache-2.0\n---\n# Reviewing Code for Security Vulnerabilities\n\n## Overview\n\nThis is a router skill. On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Never load the whole\n  library into context at once.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Every security-relevant behaviour from Step 1 is either matched to a skill and\n  checked, or explicitly reviewed against general knowledge.\n- Every finding carries a CWE id and a `file:line`.\n- The \"Skills applied\" list is present.\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

{"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_6","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,260p' /data/results/qwen/set_a/post_research/6/security/SECURITY.md\nsed -n '1,220p' /data/results/qwen/set_a/post_research/6/security/README.md\"","aggregated_output":"# Security notes\n\nCompanion to [README.md](README.md), which maps the eight rules to the code. This file records the\nattacker-facing inventory, how the guards were falsified, and what is *not* protected.\n\n## Scope\n\nWhat is in scope is the attack surface of one component: a login attempt carrying\n(username, password, source address), the credential and configuration files it reads, the audit file\nit writes, and the session identifier it returns. Account management, password reset, administration\nand monitoring are deliberately absent, so there is no admin endpoint, no recovery flow and no\nstatistics surface to attack.\n\n## Trust boundary inventory\n\nEvery value that crosses into a security decision, where it comes from, and what happens to it before\nit is used. Nothing below is trusted on arrival.\n\n| Value | Origin | Validation before use | Used for |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| username | caller (transport) | 2–64 code points; letters, digits and `._-@` only; every code point ≤ U+0020 and U+007F refused *before* any length folding; must start with letter/digit/`_`, end with letter/digit; `..` refused | throttle key (lower-cased ASCII), `account_id` hash; never written to the log as text |\n| password | caller, as `char[]` | control-character scan first (any `c < U+0021` or `c == U+007F`, so an all-NUL buffer cannot pass on size alone), then 10–256 code points, then a byte-exact UTF-8 round trip (a character that encodes to `?` is refused, because two different passwords would otherwise hash alike) | one PBKDF2 derivation; copied, wiped after encoding, wiped again in `finally` |\n| source address | caller — must be the transport's real peer address | ≤ 45 chars; no space, control, `%`, `/`, `@`, `#`; strict dotted-quad for v4 (no leading zeros, no `127.1`, no `3232235777`) or bare-hex v6 with an optional strict dotted-quad tail; parsed by `InetAddress` and re-canonicalised | per-source throttle bucket and the `source=` log field; a non-canonical form is refused as a log field |\n| accounts file | operator, path from config | size ≤ 8 MiB checked **before** reading; ≤ 20 000 records; ≤ 512 chars/line; exactly 4 colon-separated fields; name re-validated; salt and digest must be *canonical* Base64 (re-encoding must reproduce the text); iteration count plain `0-9` only — no leading zero, no `+`, no Unicode digit; iteration count must fall inside `[minAcceptedIterations, pbkdf2Iterations]`; file must be a regular file at 0600 | credential lookup. Any defect refuses startup, not the record |\n| keystore | operator, path from config | regular file, 0600 read-back, type only from the `.jceks`/`.p12`/`.pfx`/`.jks` suffix, entry must be a `SecretKeyEntry` ≥ 16 bytes | pepper for the digest |\n| config values | environment | all 16 keys required; range-checked; unknown key in the `AUTH_` namespace refused; iteration spellings above are refused as text | startup, or `InvalidConfigException` |\n| session identifier | **generated here only** — 32 lowercase hex from `SecureRandom.getStrongInstance()` | a caller-supplied suffix is validated as exactly 6 hex chars and a rejected suffix refuses the whole line rather than dropping the field | cookie value. Never accepted as input |\n\nTwo things are worth naming explicitly because they are the usual findings in this design:\n\n* `X-Forwarded-For` is not read anywhere. If a caller passes a header value as the source address, the\n  per-source budget becomes attacker-controlled — that is the caller's trust boundary, and the\n  README says so at the call site.\n* `account_id` is an unkeyed SHA-256 of the submitted name. It is one-way, so a leaked log is not a\n  list of accounts, but it is not keyed either: an attacker who already holds a candidate name list\n  can confirm membership against a stolen log. See residual risks.\n\n## Attack positions\n\n| If you are… | What you get | Why that is not enough |\n|---|---|---|\n| an unauthenticated network client | one denial with a message identical for every credential failure | existence is not in the message, the status, the retry hint, or the response time (one derivation on every credential path, then every outcome held to one floor; suite 3 asserts the wall spread and per-path CPU floors) |\n| the same, spraying one account | that account locks after N consecutive failures | the lock is per account and read-only in the check path, so spraying cannot lock someone else out; the source budget is consumed *before* credential work, so burning it costs no derivations |\n| the same, spraying many accounts from one address | each account locks independently | the per-source window is what bounds that, and its table is capped with a fail-closed verdict rather than an unbounded map |\n| a caller who can supply the source string | arbitrary bucket labels | address canonicalisation collapses the spellings of one host onto one bucket; the tables are capped at 20 000 keys, and a *new* key past the cap is BLOCKED, so inventing keys cannot evict a real bucket's state |\n| a user on the same host | nothing readable | log and keystore are created 0600 with a permission read-back; the log is reopened only after the same checks |\n| a process that replaces or unlinks the log while the service runs | an audit gap it cannot hide | the per-write identity check compares the `fileKey()` of the name against the inode the channel was opened on and refuses the write; the attempt is denied, not silently logged elsewhere. `logrotate` must therefore use `create`, not `copytruncate` |\n| someone who chmods the log while it is open | no further events | the mode is re-read per write; the moment it is not owner-only, writes stop and attempts are refused |\n| an attacker injecting through any field | no forged record | the rendered line must match a fixed grammar, is capped at 320 chars, and any newline/CR/unexpected field makes the *whole event* refused; the replacement alert keeps the account attribution rather than dropping it |\n| a caller who crashes the process mid-success | nothing to reuse | a session identifier that was issued but never handed over (because the success event failed to persist) is revoked before the method returns |\n\n## Mutation battery\n\nFunctional suites can be green because they assert the wrong thing, so each load-bearing guard was\nattacked with a one-line textual mutation and checked against the suites. A mutant counts as **killed**\nonly when a suite prints a `FAIL` line. A suite that crashed without a `FAIL` was recorded as an error\nand re-run, never counted as a kill; an anchor that no longer matched uniquely was reported as\nanchor-drift rather than silently skipped. Scripts: [`tools/mutation-battery.sh`](tools/mutation-battery.sh)\nand [`tools/mutation-timing.sh`](tools/mutation-timing.sh).\n\nSuite 3 measures timing and is therefore excluded from the main battery — a concurrently loaded machine\nmakes it flap for unrelated reasons. That exclusion is why M12 needed a second pass; the timing script\nruns it alone against suite 3.\n\n| # | Mutation | Result | Evidence / reason |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| M01 | empty-password guard `raw.length == 0` → never | survived | equivalent — see below |\n| M02 | control scan admits a space | **killed** | suite 1 `rejects a space` |\n| M03 | dotted-quad octet count `== 4` → `>= 3` | survived | equivalent — see below |\n| M04a | `isRegularFile()` branch of the per-write target check | survived | subsumed — see below |\n| M04b | `fileKey()` comparison in the same check removed | **killed** (after a fix) | suite 4 `an attempt whose log inode was replaced underneath us is refused` |\n| M05 | log permission read-back removed | **killed** (after a fix) | suite 4 `an attempt is refused once the open log becomes world-readable` |\n| M06 | session capacity cap removed | **killed** | suite 1 refusal-to-issue, suite 5 `concurrent issues stop at the cap` |\n| M07 | source table cap removed | **killed** | suite 5 `a new source is refused once the table is full` |\n| M08 | account table cap removed | **killed** | suite 5 `a new account key is refused once that table is full too` |\n| M09 | idle sweep uses the wall clock instead of the decision clock | **killed** | suite 5 `sweepingUsesTheDecisionClock` — this mutant is a real outage bug: it wiped lockout state under an injected clock |\n| M10 | unusable session id silently dropped instead of refusing the line | **killed** (after a fix) | suite 1 `a session suffix that is not hex is refused` |\n| M11 | `MessageDigest.isEqual` → `Arrays.equals` | survived both passes (suites 1, 2 and 3) | undetectable here — see below |\n| M12 | latency floor call removed | survived the main pass, **killed** by the timing pass | informative: suite 2 asserts behaviour, not duration, so it *should* miss this. Suite 3 `every credential outcome takes comparable wall time (ratio <= 1.45)` catches it |\n| M13 | credential record-count bound removed | **killed** | suite 1 `too many records is refused by name` |\n| M14 | filesystem checks skipped on the environment path | **killed** | suite 1 `refuses a keystore with permissions [OWNER_READ, OWNER_WRITE, OTHERS_READ]` |\n| M15 | orphaned session identifier never revoked | **killed** | suite 2 `the refused attempt left no live identifier behind` |\n| M16 | leading-zero iteration spelling admitted | **killed** | suite 1 `the iteration spelling [0120000] is refused` |\n| M17 | non-canonical Base64 admitted | **killed** | suite 1 `non-canonical Base64 in a record is refused` |\n| M18 | alert loses the rejected event's account attribution | **killed** | suite 4 `the alert keeps the rejected event's account attribution` |\n\nGuards added after the second adversarial review pass, each with its own mutant:\n\n| # | Mutation | Result | Evidence |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| M19 | torn tail not repaired (`boundary = offsetAfterLastNewline(...)` → `boundary = size`) | **killed** | suite 4 `the partial trailing record was discarded [expected=<82> actual=<0>]` |\n| M20 | bytes discarded on reopen are never reported | **killed** | suite 4 `the discarded bytes are reported once, as their own record` |\n| M21 | event-log size ceiling removed | **killed** | suite 4 `the bound engaged before the attempts ran out, after 30 records` |\n| M22 | a refusal that could not be logged is also charged to the account | **killed** (after a fix — see below) | suite 2 `and it is a plain denial, not an early lock: the outage attempts counted once each` |\n| M23 | the lock event contradicts the failure it follows (`present` → `false`) | **killed** | suite 4 `the lock line agrees that the account exists` |\n| M24 | environment path builds the config without the cross-field sizing rules | **killed** | suite 1 `the environment path refuses a source budget below the account lock threshold` |\n| M25 | stored work factor uncapped per attempt (`> maxSaltIterations` → `> MAX_ITERATIONS`) | **killed** | suite 1 `deriving above the configured cost is refused rather than performed` |\n| M26 | store admits a record above the configured work factor | **killed** | suite 1 `store rejects [alice:AwoRGB8mLTQ7QklQV15lbA…]` |\n| M27 | session shape check moved behind the table sweep | **killed** | suite 1 `it requires the exact identifier length` |\n| M28 | an unreadable POSIX mode accepted as owner-only | **killed** | suite 1 `a mode that cannot be read is not accepted as owner-only` |\n| M29 | an unreadable POSIX mode accepted as not exposed | **killed** | suite 1 `a mode that cannot be read counts as exposed to others` |\n\nM28 and M29 are separate mutants on purpose. One source-text assertion covers all three fail-closed\nmode predicates, so a single needle could satisfy three claims while two of them were inverted; these\ntwo mutants prove each half of that assertion is live rather than one check carrying three.\n\n**25 of 30 killed.** The main battery reported `killed=24 survived=6 errors=0`. M22 is counted as\nkilled because it died on the re-run after its falsifier was written, and M12 is killed by the timing\npass rather than the main battery — suite 2 asserts behaviour, not duration, so it *should* miss that\none. That leaves four survivors, each with a reason rather than a hand-wave:\n\n* **M01 — equivalent mutant.** With the guard removed, an empty buffer still fails\n  `raw.length < MIN_PASSWORD_CODEPOINTS` two checks later and throws the *same*\n  `PASSWORD_TOO_SHORT`. Only which guard fires changes; no observable behaviour differs, so no test can\n  kill it. The guard stays because it names the real reason.\n* **M04a — a deliberately redundant layer, and the honest version of that claim.** Deleting the\n  `isRegularFile()` branch of the per-write target check changes nothing observable, because the\n  `fileKey()` comparison immediately below rejects a replaced target for every case the branch was\n  written for — including the log-rotation case, where the rotation that changes the inode *is* a\n  replacement. So the branch is defence-in-depth, not the load-bearing guard, and M04b (removing the\n  key comparison) is the mutant that actually exercises this protection. The battery carries both so the\n  redundancy is visible rather than counted as coverage.\n* **M03 — equivalent mutant, verified by differential.** `octetsSeen` is computed with a virtual\n  terminator, and the only caller that can reach the `return` on an IPv4 path has already required\n  exactly three dots, so `octetsSeen` is exactly 4 for every input that gets there. On the IPv6 tail\n  path the relaxed count does admit short embedded quads such as `::ffff:1.2.3.4.5`, but the JDK parse\n  a few lines later refuses them: a 14-form differential run over the mapped-tail space produced **no\n  observable difference** between baseline and mutant. Caveat worth keeping: this equivalence leans on\n  `InetAddress` continuing to reject short-form embedded quads. The strict guard is the layer that does\n  not have to be re-verified per JDK, which is why it stays.\n* **M11 — undetectable by these suites, not harmless.** For the 32-byte digests compared here,\n  `Arrays.equals` and `MessageDigest.isEqual` return the same answer; the only difference is early\n  exit on the first differing byte, a handful of nanoseconds inside a ~175 ms derivation that suite 3\n  cannot resolve. This is the one surviving mutation whose real-world counterpart is a genuine (if\n  impractical here) timing channel, so it is recorded rather than argued away. `MessageDigest.isEqual`\n  stays because it is the platform's constant-time compare.\n\nFour of the killed mutants were **not** killed on the first pass, and the reason matters:\n\n* **M04/M05** survived because suite 4 only ever reached the per-write identity and mode checks *after*\n  `reopenIfPossible()` had already re-validated the same properties. The guard was real but its own\n  branch was untested — a redundant-layer artifact. Suite 4 now moves a replacement file in atomically\n  and chmods the log *while the channel is open*, isolating each check. Both die on their own assertion\n  now.\n* **M10** survived only because the battery ran it against suite 4 while its falsifier lives in suite 1.\n  A scoping error in the harness, not a gap — corrected in the battery's suite list.\n* **M22** survived the first pass with a test in place that looked like it covered the case.\n  `anAuditOutageNeverLocksOutAGoodPassword` drives five correct-password attempts during an outage and\n  asserts the account is not locked — but the success path calls `resetAccount` *before* the success\n  event is recorded, so a charge injected by the mutant was wiped a moment later and the streak never\n  grew. The double-charge is observable on the *mismatch* path, which has no reset: there the failure is\n  charged and then recorded, so the mutant halves the lock threshold.\n  `aRefusalThatCouldNotBeLoggedChargesTheAccountOnce` pins the threshold itself — after\n  `accountLockAfter - 1` mismatch attempts during an outage, the next mismatch must still be a plain\n  denial with no retry hint. That is the assertion the mutant dies on.\n* **The harness itself was unsound for source-text guards, and that was fixed first.** Several guards in\n  suite 1 assert against the *text* of shipped sources (`ProductionSource`), read through\n  `-Dauth.source.dir`. The battery originally laid its mutated copy out as a flat `src/main` tree and\n  pointed that property at it, so the property resolved to nothing and the suites silently fell back to\n  the pristine checkout: every source-text assertion would have inspected unmutated code and reported\n  the guard intact, manufacturing a survivor for each. The scratch tree is now a real\n  `<root>/src/{main,test}` layout, `ProductionSource` prefers `-Dauth.source.dir` and raises rather than\n  falling back when a file is missing, and the battery starts with a `BASELINE` case — a no-op edit that\n  must leave all four suites green — and aborts before any mutant if it does not.\n\nTwo guards are checked at the source level rather than by testing, because no schedule can force the\ninterleaving: `lockGuardIsAtomicInSource` (suite 5) reads `AttemptThrottle.java` with comments stripped\nand requires the failure counter to be incremented inside a single `ConcurrentHashMap.compute` and the\nlock-check path to consume nothing. A test for that property would be a timed race that passes for\nbroken code too.\n\n## Residual risks\n\nStated as what remains exploitable, not as caveats that the code already handles.\n\n1. **Throttle, session and audit state are per process.** Behind N replicas, an attacker gets N source\n   budgets and N account counters, and a session issued by one node is unknown to the others. This\n   component has no distributed primitive; move these three to shared storage before scaling out.\n2. **The `seq=` field orders events within one process only.** Two writers to one file both start at 1.\n   The durable content is correct; the number is not a cross-process primary key.\n3. **`account_id` is unkeyed.** Confirm a guessed name against a stolen log requires only hashing. A\n   keyed identifier (HMAC with a deployment secret) would close it; it was not added because it makes\n   the log unsearchable across deployments and the rule set did not call for it.\n4. **PBKDF2, not Argon2id.** The iteration floor (120 000, measured ~175 ms here) is enforced, but\n   PBKDF2 is not memory-hard, so a GPU/ASIC attacker gets closer to linear speedup than with\n   memory-hard KDFs. Raising `AUTH_PBKDF2_ITERATIONS` and `AUTH_MIN_ACCEPTED_ITERATIONS` together is\n   the migration path.\n5. **Timing equalisation reduces, never closes, the channel.** The floor is measured at startup on one\n   machine; per-request cost varies with the CPU, GC pauses and scheduling can widen the gap, and an\n   attacker on the same host sees noise you cannot. The design does not depend on the padding: the\n   work is equal by construction, the padding covers what is left around it.\n6. **Live session identifiers sit in the heap** in plain strings. Anyone who can read process memory\n   uses one until the TTL. `SecretKeySpec.erase()` is a no-op on this JDK (it throws and destroys\n   nothing), and `char[]`→`String` copies linger until collection, so wiping narrows a window rather\n   than sealing it.\n7. **Cookie hardening is the caller's.** `sessionCookieFor` emits `HttpOnly`, `Secure` and `SameSite`\n   only when told the connection is TLS; terminating TLS elsewhere, or passing `isTls() == false`,\n   produces a weaker cookie. Same for the 429/401 mapping — the status code itself can disclose a\n   throttle vs. a credential failure if the caller renders them differently.\n8. **Log integrity is append-integrity, not tamper evidence.** Owner-only mode keeps other users out;\n   the process's own uid can rewrite the file, and there is no hash chain. Ship it off-host if the\n   operator is in the threat model.\n9. **Availability is traded for the work budget.** Four concurrent derivations, 2 s permit wait, then\n   `WORK_UNAVAILABLE` (~20 logins/s on the reference machine). Driving load denies logins; that is the\n   intended trade against an unbounded derivation queue, but it is real impact.\n10. **`copytruncate` log rotation is invisible to the audit guard.** Truncating in place keeps the same\n    inode and mode, so the per-write identity check has nothing to detect and the logger keeps appending\n    from offset 0 — alongside the usual copytruncate gap where events written between the copy and the\n    truncate are lost from both copies. Verified directly, not reasoned: an in-place `truncate(0)` left\n    `unsavedEvents=0` and the next write succeeded. Use `create` mode instead, and **`create 0600\n    <user> <group>`** — a `0644` file left by the default `create` was measured refusing 4 of 4\n    subsequent events, because the reopen path adopts only an exactly-owner-only file. A `create 0600`\n    rotation costs exactly one refused attempt at the rotation instant and then resumes.\n# Authentication component (`com.example.auth`)\n\nUsername/password sign-in plus an append-only authentication event log. Java 17, zero third-party\ndependencies, no build tool required.\n\nScope is deliberately narrow: **verify a credential, issue a session identifier, record the\nattempt.** There is no sign-up, no password reset, no role or permission model, no admin endpoint,\nno metrics or health endpoint. The service exposes no way to create or modify a credential record;\nprovisioning is out of band (see [Provisioning](#provisioning)).\n\n---\n\n## Build and test\n\n```sh\nJAVAC=/opt/jdk17/bin/javac      # any JDK 17+\nJAVA=/opt/jdk17/bin/java\n\n$JAVAC -Xlint:all -Werror -d out/main $(find src/main/java -name '*.java')\n$JAVAC -Xlint:all -Werror -d out/test -cp out/main $(find src/test/java -name '*.java')\n\nfor c in Suite1Validation Suite2LoginBehavior Suite3Latency Suite4AuditDurability Suite5Concurrency; do\n  $JAVA -cp out/main:out/test \"com.example.auth.$c\"\ndone\n```\n\nBoth trees compile clean under `-Xlint:all -Werror`. Each suite prints `PASS`/`FAIL` per check and a\nfinal `SUMMARY <name> passed=N failed=0` line; a nonzero exit status means at least one failure.\nSuites are independent and use pid-scoped scratch directories under `$TMPDIR`, so they can run in any\norder. Run them sequentially: suite 3 measures timing, and a concurrently loaded machine can make its\nassertions flap for reasons unrelated to the code.\n\n## Using it\n\n```java\nAuthConfig config = AuthConfig.fromEnvironment(TimeSource.systemUtc());  // or AuthConfig.builder()...build()\ntry (AuthenticationService service = AuthenticationService.builder()\n        .config(config)\n        .build()) {\n\n    List<String> problems = service.selfCheck();            // operator-visible degradation\n    char[] password = request.password();                   // caller owns this array\n    try {\n        AuthResult result = service.authenticate(\n                AuthenticationService.Attempt.of(request.username(), password, request.peerAddress()));\n        if (result.isSuccess()) {\n            response.setHeader(\"Set-Cookie\",\n                    service.sessionCookieFor(result, \"auth-session\", \"/app\", request.isTls()));\n        } else {\n            response.setStatus(result.retryAfterSeconds() > 0 ? 429 : 401);\n            renderToUser(result.message());                 // identical for every credential failure\n        }\n    } finally {\n        java.util.Arrays.fill(password, (char) 0);           // required\n    }\n}\n```\n\n`Attempt.of` takes the password as a `char[]` precisely so the caller can erase it; the service makes\nits own copy, wipes that copy after encoding to UTF-8, and wipes the UTF-8 bytes in a `finally` block.\n`AuthResult.toString()` and `Attempt.toString()` never render a password or an identifier.\n\nThe peer address must be the transport's actual peer address. **Never pass an `X-Forwarded-For` value\nhere** unless a proxy you control set it; the per-source budget is only as trustworthy as that string.\n\n---\n\n## Configuration\n\n`AuthConfig.fromEnvironment(TimeSource)` reads these keys. **Every one of them is required**: there is\nno key this component will silently supply a value for, because a missing throttle or session limit\nmust stop startup rather than become an accidental default. Anything missing, malformed, out of range,\nor pointing at a file whose permissions would expose credential material stops startup with\n`InvalidConfigException`. Problem strings name keys and fixed reasons only — never a configured value,\nso the keystore password cannot reach a log or a terminal through an error path.\n\nThe only values that ever have a default are the ones you set explicitly through\n`AuthConfig.builder()`; that column below is what the builder starts from before you override it, not\nsomething the environment path will fall back to.\n\n| Key | Bounds | Builder starting value | Notes |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| `AUTH_ACCOUNTS_FILE` | absolute path, regular file, 0600 | — | |\n| `AUTH_EVENT_LOG_FILE` | absolute path; parent dir must not be other-writable | — | |\n| `AUTH_KEYSTORE_FILE` | absolute path, 0600, `.jceks`/`.p12`/`.pfx`/`.jks` | — | type taken from the suffix |\n| `AUTH_KEYSTORE_PASSWORD` | 1–512 chars | — | never stored in the built config |\n| `AUTH_PASSWORD_ENTRY_ALIAS` | `[A-Za-z0-9._-]{1,128}` | — | must name a `SecretKeyEntry` ≥ 16 bytes |\n| `AUTH_PBKDF2_ITERATIONS` | **120 000** (hard floor) – 5 000 000 | 120 000 | the floor is compiled in, not configurable; also the **ceiling** on any stored record's count |\n| `AUTH_MIN_ACCEPTED_ITERATIONS` | 10 000 – 10 000 000 | 50 000 | per-record floor, for later upgrades; must be ≤ `AUTH_PBKDF2_ITERATIONS` |\n| `AUTH_ACCOUNT_LOCK_AFTER` | 3 – 100 | 5 | consecutive failures |\n| `AUTH_ACCOUNT_LOCK_SECONDS` | 60 – 3600 | 300 | |\n| `AUTH_SOURCE_WINDOW_SECONDS` | 60 – 3600 | 300 | |\n| `AUTH_SOURCE_MAX_ATTEMPTS` | 10 – 1000 | 60 | must be ≥ `AUTH_ACCOUNT_LOCK_AFTER` |\n| `AUTH_SOURCE_BLOCK_SECONDS` | 60 – 1800 | 300 | |\n| `AUTH_SESSION_TTL_SECONDS` | 300 – 86400 | 1800 | sliding |\n| `AUTH_MAX_ACTIVE_SESSIONS` | 16 – 100 000 | 10 000 | |\n| `AUTH_MAX_EVENTS` | 100 – 200 000 | 10 000 | in-memory ring only; the file itself stops growing at a 64 MiB ceiling |\n| `AUTH_MIN_FREE_BYTES` | ≥ 1 MiB | 16 MiB | |\n\n`AuthConfig.build()` enforces two rules that relate one setting to another, and\n`fromEnvironment` constructs its config **through `build()`** rather than through the constructor, so\nneither is skippable by a deployment that writes the numbers into the environment:\n\n* `sourceMaxAttempts ≥ accountLockAfter`. That rule is the one that keeps a single attacker with one\n  known username from spending a whole shared NAT address's budget: with the account lock firing\n  first, per-source traffic from a targeted attack stays inside the source budget. Sizing for a\n  deployment behind shared egress addresses: set `AUTH_SOURCE_MAX_ATTEMPTS` to roughly\n  `accountLockAfter × (legitimate accounts per address) × 3`, and keep it well above\n  `AUTH_ACCOUNT_LOCK_AFTER`.\n* `pbkdf2Iterations ≥ minAcceptedIterations`, and a stored record's own count must fall inside\n  `[minAcceptedIterations, pbkdf2Iterations]`. The upper half is what stops a credential file from\n  ordering this process to do arbitrary work per sign-in — see\n  [rule 6](#6-failure-behaviour--generic-user-errors-detail-only-in-the-log-never-reveal-whether-an-account-exists-never-a-permissive-default).\n\n---\n\n## Rule-by-rule\n\n### 1. Trust boundaries — validate every input before use; never let an unvalidated value decide a security outcome\n\nEvery string that arrives from a client, a file, or the environment is validated before it influences\na decision, a log field, or a filesystem path.\n\n| Input | Validation (`InputValidator`) |\n|---|---|\n| username | 2–64 code points; no code point ≤ U+0020 or U+007F; must start with a letter, digit or `_`; must end with a letter or digit; `..` refused; rejects `NUL`, tab, CR, LF, and terminal escapes by the same rule |\n| password | control-character scan **first** (any `c < U+0021` or `U+007F` is refused, including an all-NUL buffer), then 10–256 code points, then an encode with `CodingErrorAction.REPORT`, so a value the platform cannot represent byte-for-byte is refused instead of substituted |\n| source address | ≤ 45 chars, no `%` scope id, no `/`, `@`, `#`, whitespace; strict dotted-quad (leading zeros refused, including in the last octet) or a hex/colon v6 form; resolved and returned in **one canonical form**, so one host always maps to one budget bucket |\n| credential file | size ≤ 8 MiB **before** it is read, ≤ 20 000 records, ≤ 512 chars/line, exactly 4 colon-separated fields, name re-validated, salt and digest must be *canonical* Base64 (re-encoding must reproduce the text), iteration count accepted only as plain `0-9` digits with no leading zero or `+`, and inside `[minAcceptedIterations, pbkdf2Iterations]` |\n| keystore path / alias / passwords | absolute regular file checked without following symlinks, not group- or other-accessible, type from an allow-listed suffix (no `file:`/jar/ZIP URL resolution), alias matched against an explicit character class and required to exist as a `SecretKeyEntry` |\n| config keys | a key name outside `[A-Z0-9_]` is reported as \"an unsupported configuration key\" rather than echoed, so a hostile environment name cannot become a terminal-control or log-injection vector |\n\nTwo details are load-bearing rather than pedantic:\n\n* **UTF-8 exactness.** `String.getBytes(UTF_8)` never throws — it folds unencodable code points to\n  `?`. Without the round-trip check, `\"café\"` and `\"caf?\"` would hash identically and a\n  constant-time comparison would report a match. Such passwords are refused with a diagnostic, not\n  silently folded.\n* **Canonical Base64.** `Base64.getDecoder()` accepts non-canonical tail bits, so two different texts\n  can decode to the same bytes. A record could then be presented in a form that no longer matches what\n  a reviewer sees in the file. Non-canonical salt *or* digest text refuses the whole store.\n\nUnvalidated values never reach a decision path: `authenticate` normalises the source address and\nrefuses the attempt (`SOURCE_REQUIRED_MESSAGE`, distinct event type, zero credential work) if it is not\na canonical literal.\n\n### 2. Secrets — none in code, never printed, logged, in argv, or in error messages or files readable by others\n\n* No credential, key, or password appears in source. The pepper comes from a password-protected\n  keystore entry at runtime; `AuthConfig.eraseSecrets()` wipes the keystore password from the built\n  config before the service is constructed, and the probe that validates the keystore uses\n  throw-away copies.\n* Nothing writes a secret to stdout/stderr. `Attempt.toString()` reports only\n  `Attempt[source=set]`; `AuthResult.toString()` omits the session identifier.\n* `AuthException`, `AuditPersistenceException` and `InvalidConfigException` carry a\n  [`Diagnostic`](src/main/java/com/example/auth/Diagnostic.java) category or a key name — never a\n  message, never a path, never a value. Their causes are deliberately suppressed, because the JDK's\n  own `IOException` and keystore exception messages quote the file path.\n* Every file this component touches is owner-only, and the check **fails closed**.\n  [`PosixMode`](src/main/java/com/example/auth/PosixMode.java) is the only place that asks what a mode\n  is, and when a file system cannot answer — CIFS/SMB/vfat, some FUSE and overlay mounts, an entry that\n  vanished between the `stat` and the read — every predicate answers *exposed*, never *safe*. Reporting\n  \"safe\" there would make an unreadable file system the one configuration where rule 2 is silently\n  dropped, which is worse than refusing to start because it looks like it passed. Concretely: the log is\n  created with `CREATE_NEW` plus an explicit 0600 `FileAttribute` and the resulting permissions are read\n  back and must be **exactly** `rw-------`; the mode is re-verified before *every* write, so a `chmod`\n  by another process stops the trail immediately rather than at the next reopen; and the credential\n  file, the keystore, the log file and the log's parent directory are all refused when group or other\n  hold any access bit (write bit, for the directory — a writable directory defeats every check made\n  against the file inside it). Suite 1 drives all three predicates into the unreadable branch with a\n  path that cannot be `stat`ed and asserts each answers \"exposed\"; suite 4 refuses a loose target\n  through the logger's own `open`, loosens the mode under a live handle, and refuses to write into a\n  replaced inode.\n* The log never contains a password (in any form), the digest, the salt, the pepper, the keystore\n  path, the whole session identifier, or the username. Suite 4 reads the real log file after logging\n  in with three different accounts and asserts the absence of all of those, including the salt and\n  digest text taken from the credential file itself.\n* Identifiers handed to a caller leave the process only through the `Set-Cookie` helper, which is\n  `HttpOnly`, `Secure`, `SameSite=Strict`, path-scoped, and `Max-Age`-bounded to the session TTL. It\n  returns `null` rather than emitting a cookie for a plaintext request.\n\n### 3. Authentication and access — verify against server-side state, fail closed, limit per source *and* per account without locking out a legitimate user\n\n* **Server-side state only.** A credential is checked against the loaded record store; a session is\n  valid only if present in this process's token table. Nothing is accepted from the caller as proof of\n  identity: no self-contained token, no `Authorization` payload, no client-supplied session id.\n* **Fail closed, in this order:** unusable source → refused; unwritable log → refused; unreadable or\n  inconsistent store → refuses to start; exhausted throttle table → refused (`BLOCKED`, not\n  \"untracked\", because an untracked key is an unlimited key); session capacity exhausted → refused;\n  permit starvation → refused after 2 s.\n* **Two independent budgets**, both applied to the same attempt:\n  * *per source* — a sliding window consumed **before any credential work**, so an attacker cannot\n    buy a PBKDF2 round per attempt beyond their budget;\n  * *per account* — a consecutive-failure lock, read-only at check time.\n* **The limit cannot be used against a legitimate user**, by four separate mechanisms:\n  1. the per-account budget only ever counts failures against *that* account, so a flood of other\n     usernames cannot consume it;\n  2. the per-source budget is configured (and validated) to be at least `accountLockAfter`, so one\n     targeted account cannot exhaust a shared address's budget — and the sizing rule above is stated\n     for shared egress;\n  3. a verified sign-in **clears** the failure state, so an attacker cannot hold an account locked by\n     generating failures around the owner's logins;\n  4. probing a locked account does **not** extend its lock — `checkAccountLock` consumes nothing and\n     `recordAccountFailure` on an already-locked bucket leaves `blockedUntilMillis` alone. Verified in\n     suite 5: a lock formed at `t` still holds at `t+599 s` and has expired at `t+601 s` no matter how\n     many failures were attempted in between.\n* Keyed on the ASCII-case-folded **submitted** name, not a resolved record. The store refuses to load\n  if two records differ only by ASCII case, so folding cannot merge two accounts; only ASCII case is\n  folded, so a Unicode look-alike (`al` + `U+00C9` + `ce`) cannot be used to lock out a real account.\n* Table sizes are capped at 20 000 keys each, with an idle sweep; a full table refuses new keys.\n  Idle stamps older than any legal window are pruned, so an attacker cannot make the process retain\n  one timestamp per attempt per address indefinitely.\n\n### 4. Identifiers and tokens — CSPRNG, never accepted from callers, strictly validated, cookies flagged\n\n* 32 lower-case hex characters = 128 bits from `SecureRandom.getStrongInstance()`, generated at the\n  moment a sign-in verifies. No counter, timestamp, MAC address, or account-derived material is\n  involved, and no identifier is ever derived from caller input.\n* Validity is presence in a server-side table. A syntactically perfect but unknown identifier is\n  simply not valid, and because nothing is parsed there is nothing to forge. Lookup is exact: an\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

     if (pepperChars == null || pepperChars.length < MIN_PEPPER_BYTES || iterations < 1) {\n    72\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n    73\t        }\n    74\t derive(byte[] passwordUtf8, byte[] salt, int saltIterations) throws AuthException {\n   104\t        if (salt == null || salt.length != SALT_BYTES) {\n   105\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n   106\t        }\n   107\t        if (saltIterations < 1 || saltIterations > maxSaltIterations) {\n   108\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n   109\t        }\n   110\t        if (passwordUtf8 == null || passwordUtf8.length == 0) {\n   111\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.PASSWORD_TOO_SHORT);\n   112\t        }\n   113\t        // PBEKeySpec takes characters, so the validated UTF-8 bytes are turned back into the\n   114\t        // ISO-8859-1 view of the same bytes. This is a bijection, so it cannot merge two distinct\n   115\t        // passwords - unlike the platform default charset, which folds unrepresentable characters.\n   116\t        char[] asChars = iso88591Chars(passwordUtf8);\n   117\t        PBEKeySpec spec = new PBEKeySpec(asChars, salt, saltIterations, DERIVED_KEY_BITS);\n   118\t        Arrays.fill(asChars, (char) 0);\n   119\t        final byte[] derived;\n   120\t        try {\n   121\t            SecretKeyFactory factory = SecretKeyFactory.getInstance(KDF_ALGORITHM);\n   122\t            derived = factory.generateSecret(spec).getEncoded();\n   123\t        } catch (GeneralSecurityException | RuntimeException e) {\n   124\t            spec.clearPassword();\n   125\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n   126\t        }\n   127\t        spec.clearPassword();\n   128\t        try {\n   129\t            Mac mac = Mac.getInstance(MAC_ALGORITHM);\n   130\t            mac.init(new SecretKeySpec(pepper, MAC_ALGORITHM));\n   131\t            mac.update(salt);\n   132\t            byte[] digest = mac.doFinal(derived);\n   133\t            Arrays.fill(derived, (byte) 0);\n   134\t            return digest;\n   135\t        } catch (GeneralSecurityException | RuntimeException e) {\n   136\t            Arrays.fill(derived, (byte) 0);\n   137\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n   138\t        }\n   139\t    }\n   140\t\n   141\t    /**\n   142\t     * Checks a candidate password against a stored digest in constant time with respect to the\n   143\t     * digest contents.\n   144\t     *\n   145\t     * @return {@code true} only when the candidate reproduces the stored digest\n   146\t     */\n   147\t    boolean matches(byte[] passwordUtf8, byte[] salt, int saltIterations, byte[] storedDigest) throws AuthException {\n   148\t        if (storedDigest == null || storedDigest.length != MAX_DIGEST_BYTES) {\n   149\t            // A truncated or oversized stored digest cannot be checked; treat as a store problem\n   150\t            // so the attempt fails closed instead of comparing against a padded try {\n   155\t            return MessageDigest.isEqual(candidate, storedDigest);\n   156\t        } finally {\n   157\t            Arrays.fill(candidate, (byte) 0);\n   158\t        }\n   159\t    }\n   160\t\n   161\t    /**\n   162\t     * Decodes a Base64 field from the credential file, requiring the exact canonical form so that\n   163\t     * two different textual values can never denote the same salt.\n   164\t     *\n   165\t     * <p>There is deliberately no matching encoder. This component reads credential records and\n   166\t     * never writes them: rendering a record is provisioning, which is account creation, and outside\n   167\t     * the scope of an authentication component.\n   168\t     *\n   169\t     * @return the decoded bytes, or {@code null} when the text is not canonical Base64\n   170\t     */\n   171\t    static byte[] decodeBytes(String value, int expectedLength) {\n   172\t        if (value == null || value.isEmpty()) {\n   173\t            return null;\n   174\t        }\n   175\t        final byte[] decoded;\n   176\t        try {\n   177\t            decoded = Base64.getDecoder().decode(value);\n   178\t        } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {\n   179\t            return null;\n   180\t        }\n   181\t        if (decoded.length != expectedLength) {\n   182\t            return null;\n   183\t        }\n   184\t        if (!Base64.getEncoder().encodeToString(decoded).equals(value)) {\n   185\t            return null;\n   186\t        }\n   187\t        return decoded;\n   188\t    }\n   189\t\n   190\t    /**\n   191\t     * Measures how long {@code iterations} of PBKDF2 take on this machine, used by the latency\n   192\t     * budget in {@link CredentialVerifier} so the budget tracks the configured work factor instead\n   193\t     * of a hard-coded guess.\n   194\t     */\n   195\t    long measureDerivationNanos(int iterations, byte[] probePassword) {\n   196\t        byte[] salt = newSalt();\n   197\t        long best = Long.MAX_VALUE;\n   198\t        for (int i = 0; i < 2; i++) {\n   199\t            long start = System.nanoTime();\n   200\t            try {\n   201\t                derive(probePassword, salt, iterations);\n   202\t            } catch (AuthException e) {\n   203\t                return -1L;\n   204\t            }\n   205\t            long elapsed = System.nanoTime() - start;\n   206\t            if (elapsed < best) {\n   207\t                best = elapsed;\n   208\t            }\n   209\t        }\n   210\t        return best;\n   211\t    }\n   212\t\n   213\t    void erase() {\n   214\t        Arrays.fill(pepper, (byte) 0);\n   215\t    }\n   216\t\n   217\t    private static char[] iso88591Chars(byte[] bytes) {\n   218\t        char[] out = new char[bytes.length];\n   219\t        for (int i = 0; i < bytes.length; i++) {\n   220\t            out[i] = (char) (bytes[i] & 0xff);\n   221\t        }\n   222\t        return out;\n   223\t    }\n   224\t\n   225\t    static byte[] utf8(String value) {\n   226\t        return value.getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);\n   227\t    }\n   228\t}\n     1\tpackage com.example.auth;\n     2\t\n     3\timport java.util.concurrent.Semaphore;\n     4\timport java.util.concurrent.TimeUnit;\n     5\t\n     6\t/**\n     7\t * Checks one credential pair and shapes the work it does so that no failure outcome is\n     8\t * distinguishable from another by timing.\n     9\t *\n    10\t * <p><b>Comparable work for every outcome.</b> Each of these paths performs one full PBKDF2\n    11\t * derivation before it returns:\n    12\t * <ul>\n    13\t *   <li>username not in the store - the derivation uses a server-generated salt with the configured\n    14\t *       iteration count, and its result is discarded;</li>\n    15\t *   <li>username present, password wrong;</li>\n    16\t *   <li>username present, password correct.</li>\n    17\t * </ul>\n    18\t * After the derivation, every remaining difference is a field read or a map probe, and the latency\n    19\t * floor absorbs the residual spread.\n    20\t *\n    21\t * <p><b>Work is capped globally, not per key.</b> A fixed number of permits, handed out in FIFO\n    22\t * order, bounds how much hashing an attacker can force regardless of how many usernames or source\n    23\t * addresses the attempts are spread across. A caller that cannot obtain a permit within\n    24\t * {@value #PERMIT_TIMEOUT_MILLIS} ms is denied; attempts are never queued without bound, because an\n    25\t * unbounded queue is the denial of service this exists to prevent. Waiting for a permit also counts\n    26\t * against the latency floor, so a saturated service is uniformly slower - it cannot be made to run\n    27\t * one outcome fast and another slow.\n    28\t *\n    29\t * <p><b>No latch.</b> Saturating the permits denies only the attempts that arrive while saturated;\n    30\t * permits are released in a {@code finally} block and the next attempt proceeds normally.\n    31\t */\n    32\tfinal class CredentialVerifier {\n    33\t\n    34\t    /** Longest a caller will wait for a work permit before being denied. */\n    35\t    static final long PERMIT_TIMEOUT_MILLIS = 2_000L;\n    36\t\n    37\t    private final PasswordHasher hasher;\n    38\t    private final LatencyBudget budget;\n    39\t    private final Semaphore workPermits;\n    40\t    private final int maxConcurrentDerivations;\n    41\t    private final AttemptParameters decoy;\n    42\t\n    43\t    CredentialVerifier(PasswordHasher hasher, LatencyBudget budget, int maxConcurrentDerivations,\n    44\t            AttemptParameters decoy) {\n    45\t        if (maxConcurrentDerivations < 1) {\n    46\t            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"maxConcurrentDerivations\");\n    47\t        }\n    48\t        this.hasher = hasher;\n    49\t        this.budget = budget;\n    50\t        this.workPermits = new Semaphore(maxConcurrentDerivations, true);\n    51\t        this.maxConcurrentDerivations = maxConcurrentDerivations;\n    52\t        this.decoy = decoy;\n    53\t    }\n    54\t\n    55\t    /**\n    56\t     * Verifies a password against the record for a resolved username.\n    57\t     *\n    58\t     * @param passwordUtf8 validated UTF-8 bytes of the candidate password\n    59\t     * @param record the record for the resolved username, or {@code null} when there is none\n    60\t     * @return {@code true} only when the digest matches\n    61\t     * @throws AuthException when the check could not be performed at all - no permit, no decoy\n    62\t     *     parameters, or a store failure. The caller must deny the attempt and must not treat this\n    63\t     *     as either a match or a distinguishable kind of failure.\n    64\t     */\n    65\t    boolean verify(byte[] passwordUtf8, AccountCredentials record) throws AuthException {\n    66\t        if (passwordUtf8 == null || passwordUtf8.length == 0) {\n    67\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.PASSWORD_TOO_SHORT);\n    68\t        }\n    69\t        if (decoy == null) {\n    70\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n    71\t        }\n    72\t        boolean acquired;\n    73\t        try {\n    74\t            acquired = workPermits.tryAcquire(PERMIT_TIMEOUT_MILLIS, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS);\n    75\t        } catch (InterruptedException e) {\n    76\t            Thread.currentThread().interrupt();\n    77\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.WORK_UNAVAILABLE);\n    78\t        }\n    79\t        if (!acquired) {\n    80\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.WORK_UNAVAILABLE);\n    81\t        }\n    82\t        long started = System.nanoTime();\n    83\t        try {\n    84\t            final boolean matched;\n    85\t            if (record == null) {\n    86\t                // Discarded deliberately: the purpose is that an unknown username costs exactly what\n    87\t                // a known one costs.\n    88\t                hasher.matches(passwordUtf8, decoy.salt(), decoy.iterations(), decoy.digest());\n    89\t                matched = false;\n    90\t            } else {\n    91\t                matched = hasher.matches(passwordUtf8, record.salt(), record.iterations(), record.digest());\n    92\t            }\n    93\t            budget.padUntil(started);\n    94\t            return matched;\n    95\t        } finally {\n    96\t            workPermits.release();\n    97\t        }\n    98\t    }\n    99\t\n   100\t    /** Derivations the service will run at once. */\n   101\t    int maxConcurrentDerivations() {\n   102\t        return maxConcurrentDerivations;\n   103\t    }\n   104\t\n   105\t    /** Salt, iteration count and digest used to burn work for an unknown username. */\n   106\t    static final class AttemptParameters {\n   107\t\n   108\t        private final byte[] salt;\n   109\t        private final int iterations;\n   110\t        private final byte[] digest;\n   111\t\n   112\t        AttemptParameters(byte[] salt, int iterations, byte[] digest) {\n   113\t            this.salt = salt;\n   114\t            this.iterations = iterations;\n   115\t            this.digest = digest;\n   116\t        }\n   117\t\n   118\t        byte[] salt() {\n   119\t            return salt.clone();\n   120\t        }\n   121\t\n   122\t        int iterations() {\n   123\t            return iterations;\n   124\t        }\n   125\t\n   126\t        byte[] digest() {\n   127\t            return digest.clone();\n   128\t        }\n   129\t    }\n   130\t}\n     1\tpackage com.example.auth;\n     2\t\n     3\timport java.io.IOException;\n     4\timport java.io.InputStream;\n     5\timport java.nio.file.Files;\n     6\timport java.nio.file.LinkOption;\n     7\timport java.nio.file.Path;\n     8\timport java.security.GeneralSecurityException;\n     9\timport java.security.KeyStore;\n    10\t\n    11\t/**\n    12\t * Reads the credential-hash pepper from a JCEKS or PKCS12 keystore using the JDK's own\n    13\t * {@link KeyStore} API.\n    14\t *\n    15\t * <p>The pepper is the platform's answer to \"where does a secret come from\": a protected entry in a\n    16\t * password-protected keystore file, not a constant in source. Nothing here accepts a secret from a\n    17\t * client, and nothing here prints one: the loaded characters are copied straight into\n    18\t * {@link PasswordHasher}, and every failure path reports only a category.\n    19\t *\n    20\t * <p>Guards applied to the attacker-controllable inputs (keystore path, entry alias, both\n    21\t * passwords):\n    22\t * <ul>\n    23\t *   <li>the path must be an absolute path to an existing regular file, checked without following\n    24\t *       symlinks, and must not be readable or writable by group or other users;</li>\n    25\t *   <li>the file type is taken from the configured suffix and must be {@code .jceks} or\n    26\t *       {@code .p12}/{@code .pfx}; an arbitrary {@code file://}, jar or ZIP URL is never\n    27\t *       resolved;</li>\n    28\t *   <li>the alias must match {@code [A-Za-z0-9._-]{1,128}} and must exist in the store;</li>\n    29\t *   <li>the entry must be a {@link KeyStore.SecretKeyEntry} whose encoded key is at least\n    30\t *       {@link PasswordHasher#MIN_PEPPER_BYTES} bytes; a private-key or certificate entry is\n    31\t *       refused.</li>\n    32\t * </ul>\n    33\t *\n    34\t * <p>A wrong keystore password surfaces from the JDK as either a {@code GeneralSecurityException}\n    35\t * or an {@code IOException}, so both are handled; the JDK message can quote the keystore path, so\n    36\t * no exception message is propagated.\n    37\t */\n    38\tfinal class KeystorePepperProvider {\n    39\t\n    40\t    private static final int MAX_ALIAS_CHARS = 128;\n    41\t\n    42\t    private KeystorePepperProvider() {\n    43\t    }\n    44\t\n    45\t    /**\n    46\t     * Loads the pepper.\n    47\t     *\n    48\t     * @return the pepper characters, which the caller must wipe after handing them to\n    49\t     *     {@link PasswordHasher}\n    50\t     * @throws AuthException when the keystore or the entry cannot be used; the diagnostic carries no\n    51\t     *     detail beyond the fixed {@link Diagnostic#STORE_UNAVAILABLE} text\n    52\t     */\n    53\t    static char[] loadPepper(Path keystoreFile, char[] keystorePassword, String alias) throws AuthException {\n    54\t        if (keystoreFile == null || keystorePassword == null || keystorePassword.length == 0) {\n    55\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n    56\t        }\n    57\t        if (!isUsableAlias(alias)) {\n    58\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n    59\t        }\n    60\t        if (!Files.isRegularFile(keystoreFile, LinkOption.NOFOLLOW_LINKS) || !Files.isReadable(keystoreFile)) {\n    61\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n    62\t        }\n    63\t        String type = keystoreType(keystoreFile);\n    64\t        if (type == null) {\n    65\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n    66\t        }\n    67\t        char[] passwordCopy = keystorePassword.clone();\n    68\t        char[] pepper = null;\n    69\t        try {\n    70\t            KeyStore store = KeyStore.getInstance(type);\n    71\t            try (InputStream in = Files.newInputStream(keystoreFile)) {\n    72\t                store.load(in, passwordCopy);\n    73\t            }\n    74\t            // PasswordProtection clones the characters it is given, so the copy can be wiped as\n    75\t            // soon as the object exists. The same password protects the entry: the two are read\n    76\t            // from one configuration key and there is no second secret to keep.\n    77\t            KeyStore.ProtectionParameter protection = new KeyStore.PasswordProtection(passwordCopy);\n    78\t            java.util.Arrays.fill(passwordCopy, (char) 0);\n    79\t            if (!store.containsAlias(alias)) {\n    80\t                throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n    81\t            }\n    82\t            KeyStore.Entry entry = store.getEntry(alias, protection);\n    83\t            // SecretKeyEntry is the only secret entry type the JDK offers, and the one keytool\n    84\t            // produces for a symmetric secret. A PrivateKeyEntry or TrustedCertificateEntry is not a\n    85\t            // MAC key and is refused rather than coerced into one.\n    86\t            if (!(entry instanceof KeyStore.SecretKeyEntry)) {\n    87\t                throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n    88\t            }\n    89\t            byte[] encoded = ((KeyStore.SecretKeyEntry) entry).getSecretKey().getEncoded();\n    90\t            if (encoded == null || encoded.length < PasswordHasher.MIN_PEPPER_BYTES) {\n    91\t                if (encoded != null) {\n    92\t                    java.util.Arrays.fill(encoded, (byte) 0);\n    93\t                }\n    94\t                throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n    95\t            }\n    96\t            // ISO-8859-1 view of the key bytes, so an arbitrary 16+ byte key works. PasswordHasher\n    97\t            // re-encodes with the same mapping, which is a bijection and cannot lose bytes.\n    98\t            char[] asChars = new char[encoded.length];\n    99\t            for (int i = 0; i < encoded.length; i++) {\n   100\t                asChars[i] = (char) (encoded[i] & 0xff);\n   101\t            }\n   102\t            java.util.Arrays.fill(encoded, (byte) 0);\n   103\t            pepper = asChars;\n   104\t            return pepper;\n   105\t        } catch (AuthException e) {\n   106\t            throw e;\n   107\t        } catch (GeneralSecurityException | IOException | RuntimeException e) {\n   108\t            throw new AuthException(Diagnostic.STORE_UNAVAILABLE);\n   109\t        } finally {\n   110\t            java.util.Arrays.fill(passwordCopy, (char) 0);\n   111\t        }\n   112\t    }\n   113\t\n   114\t    static boolean isUsableAlias(String alias) {\n   115\t        if (alias == null || alias.isEmpty() || alias.length() > MAX_ALIAS_CHARS) {\n   116\t            return false;\n   117\t        }\n   118\t        for (int i = 0; i < alias.length(); i++) {\n   119\t            char c = alias.charAt(i);\n   120\t            boolean allowed = (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') || (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') || (c >= '0' && c <= '9')\n   121\t                    || c == '-' || c == '_' || c == '.';\n   122\t            if (!allowed) {\n   123\t                return false;\n   124\t            }\n   125\t        }\n   126\t        return true;\n   127\t    }\n   128\t\n   129\t    /** Maps the file suffix onto a keystore type; anything unrecognised is refused. */\n   130\t    static String keystoreType(Path file) {\n   131\t        String name = file.getFileName().toString().toLowerCase(java.util.Locale.ROOT);\n   132\t        if (name.endsWith(\".jceks\")) {\n   133\t            return \"JCEKS\";\n   134\t        }\n   135\t        if (name.endsWith(\".p12\") || name.endsWith(\".pfx\")) {\n   136\t            return \"PKCS12\";\n   137\t        }\n   138\t        if (name.endsWith(\".jks\")) {\n   139\t            return \"JKS\";\n   140\t        }\n   141\t        return null;\n   142\t    }\n   143\t}\n     1\tpackage com.example.auth;\n     2\t\n     3\timport java.security.SecureRandom;\n     4\timport java.util.Iterator;\n     5\timport java.util.LinkedHashMap;\n     6\timport java.util.Map;\n     7\t\n     8\t/**\n     9\t * Issues and validates session identifiers.\n    10\t *\n    11\t * <p>Identifiers are 32 characters of lower-case hexadecimal - 128 bits from\n    12\t * {@link SecureRandom#getStrongInstance()} - generated by this code at the moment a sign-in is\n    13\t * verified. They are never accepted from a caller as an input to sign-in, never derived from\n    14\t * anything the caller supplies, and never predictable: there is no counter, timestamp, MAC address\n    15\t * or hash-of-the-account involved.\n    16\t *\n    17\t * <p>Validity is decided only by presence in this table, which is server-side state. A\n    18\t * well-formed-but-unknown identifier is simply not valid; there is no self-contained token to trust\n    19\t * and nothing to parse, so a caller cannot construct one.\n    20\t *\n    21\t * <p><b>Atomicity.</b> Issue, validate-and-touch, revoke and capacity bookkeeping all happen under\n    22\t * one lock on the map. Expired entries are dropped inside the same critical section as a validation,\n    23\t * so an expired session cannot be validated by one thread while another is still touching it.\n    24\t *\n    25\t * <p><b>Bounded.</b> At most {@code maxActiveSessions} identifiers exist; when the table is full the\n    26\t * oldest-expiring entry is evicted and a fresh issue is refused only if no entry can be reclaimed.\n    27\t * Refusing to issue (rather than dropping capacity limits) is what keeps memory bounded under a\n    28\t * script that signs in continuously.\n    29\t *\n    30\t * <p><b>Residual risk, documented in the README:</b> the table is in-process. Behind more than one\n    31\t * node, a session issued by one node is unknown to another; a deployment needs a shared session\n    32\t * store. In-memory identifiers are also not protected at rest - they live in the heap, which is why\n    33\t * the README lists heap dumping as a residual risk and why the transport requirements below apply.\n    34\t *\n    35\t * <p><b>Cookie contract for the caller.</b> This component returns the identifier only; the caller\n    36\t * must set it as a cookie with {@code HttpOnly}, {@code Secure}, {@code SameSite=Strict}, a\n    37\t * {@code Path} no broader than the application root, and an {@code Expires}/{@code Max-Age} no\n    38\t * longer than {@link #ttlSeconds()}. It must not place the identifier in a URL, in a response body,\n    39\t * in a log line, or in a {@code Referrer-Policy}-permitted link. A helper that builds the\n    40\t * {@code Set-Cookie} value is provided in {@link #cookieDirectiveFor(String, String, String, boolean)}\n    41\t * so the flags cannot be forgotten by an integrator; it emits {@code HttpOnly}, {@code Secure} and\n    42\t * {@code SameSite=Strict} on a TLS request and returns {@code null} for a plaintext one, so a caller\n    43\t * that cannot say whether the connection is TLS gets no cookie at all rather than one that could\n    44\t * travel in clear text. The four-argument form is the only one an integrator should reach for: the\n    45\t * three-argument overload is a convenience for code that has already proven it is on TLS, and it\n    46\t * asserts {@code secureTransport = true} on the caller's behalf.\n    47\t */\n    48\tfinal class SessionTokenStore {\n    49\t\n    50\t    /** Hex characters: 32 of them encode 128 bits. */\n    51\t    static final int TOKEN_HEX_LENGTH = 32;\n    52\t\n    53\t    private static final long MIN_EVICTION_GRACE_MILLIS = 1_000L;\n    54\t\n    55\t    private final SecureRandom random;\n    56\t    private final int ttlSeconds;\n    57\t    private final int maxActiveSessions;\n    58\t    private final TimeSource time;\n    59\t    private final LinkedHashMap<String, Long> expiresAtMillis = new LinkedHashMap<>();\n    60\t\n    61\t    private long issuedCount;\n    62\t    private long evictedCount;\n    63\t\n    64\t    SessionTokenStore(SecureRandom random, int ttlSeconds, int maxActiveSessions, TimeSource time) {\n    65\t        this.random = random;\n    66\t        this.ttlSeconds = ttlSeconds;\n    67\t        this.maxActiveSessions = maxActiveSessions;\n    68\t        this.time = time;\n    69\t    }\n    70\t\n    71\t    /**\n    72\t     * Issues a new identifier.\n    73\t     *\n    74\t     * @return the identifier, or {@code null} when capacity is exhausted and nothing could be\n    75\t     *     reclaimed. The caller must then fail the sign-in: issuing beyond capacity is not an\n    76\t     *     option, and neither is silently evicting a live user's session to make room.\n    77\t     */\n    78\t    synchronized String issue() {\n    79\t        long now = time.nowMillis();\n    80\t        dropExpiredLocked(now);\n    81\t        if (expiresAtMillis.size() >= maxActiveSessions) {\n    82\t            if (!evictOldestExpiringLocked(now)) {\n    83\t                return null;\n    84\t            }\n    85\t            evictedCount++;\n    86\t        }\n    87\t        String token = newToken();\n    88\t        expiresAtMillis.put(token, now + ttlSeconds * 1000L);\n    89\t        issuedCount++;\n    90\t        return token;\n    91\t    }\n    92\t\n    93\t    /**\n    94\t     * Validates an identifier and slides its expiry on success. Validity is presence in this table\n    95\t     * and nothing else.\n    96\t     *\n    97\t     * <p>The shape check runs <em>first</em>, outside the lock. Everything a caller can send arrives\n    98\t     * unauthenticated, and a table scan on the far side of that check would let anyone holding a\n    99\t     * keyboard convert the expiry sweep into a cost that scales with the deployment's session cap\n   100\t     * (100&nbsp;000 entries) rather than with the traffic they can actually generate. The sweep is\n   101\t     * therefore reached only by a string that is already the right shape - i.e. one that is far more\n   102\t     * likely to be a real identifier than an attacker's first probe.\n   103\t     */\n   104\t    synchronized boolean isValid(String candidate) {\n   105\t        if (candidate == null || candidate.length() != TOKEN_HEX_LENGTH || !isHex(candidate)) {\n   106\t            return false;\n   107\t        }\n   108\t        long now = time.nowMillis();\n   109\t        dropExpiredLocked(now);\n   110\t        // The lookup is a hash probe: no byte-by-byte comparison against a stored identifier ever\n   111\t        // happens, so there is nothing for a timing measurement to follow. Guessing is ruled out by\n   112\t        // the 128-bit space, not by comparison timing.\n   113\t        Long expiry = expiresAtMillis.get(candidate);\n   114\t        if (expiry == null || expiry <= now) {\n   115\t            return false;\n   116\t        }\n   117\t        expiresAtMillis.put(candidate, now + ttlSeconds * 1000L);\n   118\t        return true;\n   119\t    }\n   120\t\n   121\t    /** Revokes an identifier. Idempotent; unknown identifiers are ignored. */\n   122\t    synchronized void revoke(String candidate) {\n   123\t        if (candidate == null || candidate.length() != TOKEN_HEX_LENGTH) {\n   124\t            return;\n   125\t        }\n   126\t        expiresAtMillis.remove(candidate);\n   127\t    }\n   128\t\n   129\t    /** Number of live identifiers, after dropping expired ones. */\n   130\t    synchronized int activeCount() {\n   131\t        dropExpiredLocked(time.nowMillis());\n   132\t        return expiresAtMillis.size();\n   133\t    }\n   134\t\n   135\t    int ttlSeconds() {\n   136\t        return ttlSeconds;\n   137\t    }\n   138\t\n   139\t    int maxActiveSessions() {\n   140\t        return maxActiveSessions;\n   141\t    }\n   142\t\n   143\t    synchronized long issuedCount() {\n   144\t        return issuedCount;\n   145\t    }\n   146\t\n   147\t    synchronized long evictedCount() {\n   148\t        return evictedCount;\n   149\t    }\n   150\t\n   151\t    synchronized void clear() {\n   152\t        expiresAtMillis.clear();\n   153\t    }\n   154\t\n   155\t    /**\n   156\t     * Convenience form for a caller that has already established the request is on TLS.\n   157\t     *\n   158\t     * <p>It asserts {@code secureTransport = true}, so on a plaintext request the browser discards\n   159\t     * the cookie rather than the session travelling in clear text - the failure is a lost sign-in, not\n   160\t     * an exposure. Integrators who cannot prove the transport should call the four-argument form and\n   161\t     * pass what they actually know.\n   162\t     *\n   163\t     * @param candidate the identifier returned by {@link AuthResult#sessionId()}\n   164\t     * @param cookieName must be a bare token: {@code [A-Za-z0-9_-]{1,64}}\n   165\t     * @param path must be an absolute path of at most 128 characters, no control characters, no\n   166\t     *     comma, semicolon or CR/LF\n   167\t     * @return the directive, or {@code null} when any input is unusable\n   168\t     */\n   169\t    public String cookieDirectiveFor(String candidate, String cookieName, String path) {\n   170\t        return cookieDirectiveFor(candidate, cookieName, path, true);\n   171\t    }\n   172\t\n   173\t    /**\n   174\t     * Builds the {@code Set-Cookie} value for a session identifier, with the flags an integrator is\n   175\t     * most likely to forget.\n   176\t     *\n   177\t     * <p>{@code Secure} is included only when {@code secureTransport} is {@code true}. A caller that\n   178\t     * cannot say whether the request arrived over TLS gets no directive at all rather than a\n   179\t     * cookie that could travel in clear text; that is the fail-closed choice.\n   180\t     *\n   181\t     * @param candidate the identifier returned by {@link AuthResult#sessionId()}\n   182\t     * @param cookieName must be a bare token: {@code [A-Za-z0-9_-]{1,64}}\n   183\t     * @param path must be an absolute path of at most 128 characters, no control characters, no\n   184\t     *     comma, semicolon or CR/LF\n   185\t     * @param secureTransport whether the request arrived over TLS; {@code false} yields {@code null}\n   186\t     * @return the directive, or {@code null} when any input is unusable or the transport is not TLS\n   187\t     */\n   188\t    public String cookieDirectiveFor(String candidate, String cookieName, String path, boolean secureTransport) {\n   189\t        if (!secureTransport) {\n   190\t            return null;\n   191\t        }\n   192\t        if (candidate == null || !isHex(candidate)) {\n   193\t            return null;\n   194\t        }\n   195\t        if (candidate.length() != TOKEN_HEX_LENGTH) {\n   196\t            return null;\n   197\t        }\n   198\t        if (!isCookieName(cookieName)) {\n   199\t            return null;\n   200\t        }\n   201\t        if (!isCookiePath(path)) {\n   202\t            return null;\n   203\t        }\n   204\t        return cookieName + \"=\" + candidate\n   205\t                + \"; Path=\" + path\n   206\t                + \"; Max-Age=\" + ttlSeconds\n   207\t                + \"; HttpOnly; SameSite=Strict; Secure\";\n   208\t    }\n   209\t\n   210\t    private static boolean isCookieName(String name) {\n   211\t        if (name == null || name.isEmpty() || name.length() > 64) {\n   212\t            return false;\n   213\t        }\n   214\t        for (int i = 0; i < name.length(); i++) {\n   215\t            char c = name.charAt(i);\n   216\t            boolean allowed = (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') || (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') || (c >= '0' && c <= '9')\n   217\t                    || c == '_' || c == '-';\n   218\t            if (!allowed) {\n   219\t                return false;\n   220\t            }\n   221\t        }\n   222\t        return true;\n   223\t    }\n   224\t\n   225\t    private static boolean isCookiePath(String path) {\n   226\t        if (path == null || path.length() < 1 || path.length() > 128 || path.charAt(0) != '/') {\n   227\t            return false;\n   228\t        }\n   229\t        for (int i = 0; i < path.length(); i++) {\n   230\t            char c = path.charAt(i);\n   231\t            if (c <= 0x20 || c == 0x7f || c == ';' || c == ',' || c == '\"' || c == '\\\\') {\n   232\t                return false;\n   233\t            }\n   234\t        }\n   235\t        return true;\n   236\t    }\n   237\t\n   238\t    private static boolean isHex(String value) {\n   239\t        for (int i = 0; Callers\n    43\t     *     must not treat {@code null} as \"no exposure\" - use the predicates below instead.\n    44\t     */\n    45\t    static Set<PosixFilePermission> permissionsOf(Path path) {\n    46\t        if (path == null) {\n    47\t            return null;\n    48\t        }\n    49\t        try {\n    50\t            return Files.getPosixFilePermissions(path, LinkOption.NOFOLLOW_LINKS);\n    51\t        } catch (IOException | RuntimeException e) {\n    52\t            return null;\n    53\t        }\n    54\t    }\n    55\t\n    56\t    /**\n    57\t     * {@code true} when {@code path} is readable, writable or executable by group or other users,\n    58\t     * or when its mode cannot be verified. That includes not\n    17\t * shipping even a package-private helper that hashes a supplied password into a stored record: such a\n    18\t * helper is account creation under another name, and a reviewer would be right to report it as\n    19\t * scope creep the moment it exists in production code. Tests build their own credential files in\n    20\t * {@code FixtureProvisioner}, against the same {@link PasswordHasher} this service verifies with, so\n    21\t * a fixture record still exercises the loader instead of restating it.\n    22\t *\n    23\t * <h2>Order of operations for one attempt</h2>\n    24\t * <ol>\n    25\t *   <li><b>Source address.</b> The caller-supplied address is parsed textually into canonical form\n    26\t *       by {@link InputValidator#normalizeSourceAddress}. A missing or unparseable address ends the\n    27\t *       attempt immediately: without it the attempt cannot be rate limited, and the throttle is part\n    28\t *       of the authentication decision. The answer is a fixed \"authentication is not available for\n    29\t *       this request\" that names no account, so it does not leak existence.</li>\n    30\t *   <li><b>Per-source budget.</b> Consumed before any credential work, so an attacker cannot buy a\n    31\t *       full PBKDF2 round per attempt beyond their budget.</li>\n    32\t *   <li><b>Per-account lock.</b> Applied read-only: probing a locked account cannot lengthen the\n    33\t *       lock, and the account's budget is never charged by another account's traffic.</li>\n    34\t *   <li><b>Validation</b> of the username and password shapes. Authentication does not proceed unrecorded.</li>\n    41\t *   <li><b>Latency floor</b> ({@link LatencyBudget}) makes every outcome above take comparable\n    42\t *       time.</li>\n    43\t *   <li><b>Credential clearing.</b> The password copy held by this component is wiped in a\n    44\t *       {@code finally} block, whatever happened.</li>\n    45\t * </ol>\n    46\t *\n    47\t * <p><b>What a caller may learn.</b> Exactly three things: the generic denial text, the same generic\n    48\t * denial text with a retry hint, or success plus a session identifier.
